# Unfilter digest 07 — baby and toddler sleep, 0–36 months

2026-11-16. All 1,424 quote records read: voice/reddit-ur03.jsonl (394), voice/reddit-ur06.jsonl (332), voice/reddit-ur20.jsonl (394), and voice/reddit-ur22.jsonl (304), plus each matching ledger’s “What these posts add” section. Comparison: README.md and current synthesis/findings.md, including the 2026-11-16 restoration and §§15–17.

These are selected, previously excluded quotations, not a prevalence sample. Counts count quote records, never people; several records can belong to one post or a later reply. Diagnoses, causal explanations, risk judgements, professional instructions and claimed results remain the speakers’ accounts. No subject is withheld or softened. This is research evidence, not a recommendation of the practices described.

Each numbered pattern names its destination, gives its relation to current conclusions, quotes current wording and states what should change. Distinctive quotations reproduce the entire quote field, including typos, punctuation, whitespace and line breaks. Repeated evidence links to its first full appearance; further supporting IDs follow each panel. Money and Requests are separate indexes, not assumed demand for a Mamivio plan.

The ledgers’ specific additions are retained: the hospital/police correction; medical and feeding trajectories; grief and prior loss; sensory regulation and protected sleep; failed or distrusted handovers; reported violence and restraint; fathers’ own distress; body boundaries; device reassurance and contested readings; and later replies that revise apparent causes. Ledger interpretations did not replace reading the records.

Possible-promotion uncertainty remains labelled: [sbu06-0192], [sbu06-0193] and [sbu06-0194] belong to a consultant-success account tagged possible_promo. These remain ordinary evidence; the tag establishes neither promotional intent nor an independent treatment result.

## 1. Pain, illness, feeding difficulty and medical uncertainty can organize the whole sleep problem.

**Findings destination:** §3, Professional advice, medical uncertainty and treatment experiences; §4.5; §§17.2 and 17.9.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Each account reports what the speaker says happened; the research conclusion is that health, feeding and access shape the sleep question.”

Keep the broader conclusion, but add the actual trajectories: cranial reconstruction, multiple heart operations and cardiology travel, seizures and an inoperable brain mass, clubfoot casts/boots/bar, prematurity after NICU, eczema, recurrent infection, feeding aversion and sleep-study referral. Treatment can relieve one symptom without restoring sleep; a medicine trial, diet change or surgery does not establish what caused later waking. Show the conflicting advice and concurrent interventions. Vomiting in twins was attributed to undiagnosed celiac disease; a different parent interprets finger-gagging as intentional. Do not merge those explanations or infer intent. Keep alternative remedies and the parent's whiskey/Baal remark in their words.

**Verbatim evidence**

<a id="q-sbu03-0031"></a>
**[sbu03-0031]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0031 -->
> Starting to try to help my 10 month old sleep independently. The past 3 months have been absolutely brutal- first, recovering from a full cranial reconstruction where, due to pain and swelling, bb often woke every 30 mins to 1 hour crying. When recovery did not continue on track, I realized that this poor thing was working on so many teeth. Now, three months later, there have been 2-3 times where I slept for four hours straight, but mostly it’s every 1-2 hours waking up crying. At 10 months old, all 8 of the first wave of teeth have already broken through the gums, and I am terrified to say that it seems like the next two upper ones might also be on the way very early.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0044"></a>
**[sbu03-0044]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0044 -->
> My daughter is two, and truthfully was the sweetest little baby from birth. She started sleeping through the night at three months, was always happy, quite literally never cried. All of a sudden she has multiple seizures, and they find a large mass on her brain that’s inoperable. She stops sleeping. She’s constantly angry. Dr’s can’t do much. I haven’t slept in months.. I’ve gotten an hour here, or three hours there, but truthfully haven’t gotten good sleep. I’m a single parent. I can’t expect anyone to help me, but I am crying out for help. I need mental help. I am self isolating and being rude to everyone around me.. I’m not okay and don’t know what to do.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0266"></a>
**[sbu03-0266]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0266 -->
> She does have more of a complex medical history (two heart surgeries, waiting for 3rd between 9-12 months) but is doing super well with her condition. That being said, we have to do monthly really tough travel days to see her cardiologist (4 hours away) which seem to ruin things.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0303"></a>
**[sbu03-0303]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0303 -->
> At a big loss here with my 19-month old! End of June, he had his adenoids out and is thankfully doing so much better with his breathing and snoring. Unfortunately that coupled with a bout of RSV has really put our sleep in the bin. We fell into the co-sleeping trap when he was recovering.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0304"></a>
**[sbu03-0304]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0304 -->
> I think in our instance, he could very well be under-tired because he’s likely sleeping better because he’s breathing better? We’ve been on the same schedule for the last 5 or so months, and it was working up until his procedure. I’ll try and put him down close to 8, because I don’t think he’ll be okay with just the 1 hour nap! We’ll try and fiddle with it. Last night was actually the first time he didn’t wake at 10:30, but woke at 3! He’d had a 2.25 hour nap at daycare 🤣 so who the heck knows!
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0306"></a>
**[sbu03-0306]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0306 -->
> to preface. my baby was born with clubfoot.. started with full leg casts at 2 weeks old, had surgery at 8 weeks, another set of casts, and now she’s in boots and bar. the treatment has made our sleeping arrangements so difficult. on top of that, she had really bad reflux and was medicated for 3 months after trying every other option. thankfully she finally outgrew that.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0357"></a>
**[sbu03-0357]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0357 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu03-0358"></a>
**[sbu03-0358]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0358 -->

Sometimes I just go in and rub their back, other times they will not settle down until they come into our room. They’ve been doing better with going back to bed with some attention in their room, which has been a major shift.
<!-- /quote -->

It’s only been a few days since we started the elimination diet, and so far, we haven’t noticed any improvement in his reflux symptoms or sleep.
<!-- /quote -->

> We have been sitting him up after eating, we burp him, we do gas drops all day every day, his naps are only sleeping on our chest sitting up, his bed is propped up in the crib, etc. He still isn't sleeping well at night due to his reflux. The first night he slept so well. He did a 5 hour stretch. Then it went downhill after that where he was waking up screaming throughout the night and not sleeping well anymore. <!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu06-0173"></a>
**[sbu06-0173]**

<!-- quote:sbu06-0173 -->
> All this led us to conclude he has silent reflux, GP agreed and has prescribed Gaviscon. He’s having 1 sachet twice a day, one morning one night in a bottle, but so far it has not made any difference. I can increase these but I feel like it isn’t likely to do much, since my understanding of Gaviscon infant is that it isn’t a drug that “builds up” in the system and changes the stomach physiology (i.e. like omeprazole does for the pH) but instead works on a per-feed basis at thickening up that feed and making it harder for it to reflux. If he has still cried intensely after each feed I’ve used Gaviscon, I don’t see how using it on more feeds will make any difference? Sometimes going out in the pram on a bumpy road makes him sleep (odd, because in doing this he is laid flat, but it sometimes works).

## 2. Night feeds and feeding to sleep can provide calories or comfort, and removing them does not reliably remove waking.

**Findings destination:** §3; §§4.2 and 4.5; §§6.1–6.2; §§10 and 17.2.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “plus questions about whether a medical need is being mistaken for a habit.”

Keep sleep onset, night waking, nutritional intake, comfort nursing and weaning as separate outcomes. Add self-weaning without better nights, worse transfers after weaning, water versus milk giving different stretches, and reinstated feeds in retraining logs. Include clinician/dietician-directed night calories, a baby able to take full feeds mainly asleep, and a reflux-related feeding aversion where sleep feeds provide growth intake. A parent asking whether a night feed is a habit has not established that it is. Parents can want one or two retained feeds rather than zero.

## 3. The caregiver's body and tolerance for contact can make an otherwise effective settling technique unusable.

**Findings destination:** §9.1, The parent’s body and health can set the limit; §§4.6, 6.2 and 10.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “a caregiver may be physically unable to sustain rocking, holding or a walking-based solution.”

Make the limit specific: back surgery prevents crib lifts; C-section wounds and stairs prevent pram walks; wrists, hands and joints hurt; a growing baby outgrows chest-sleeping comfort. Feeding contact can become intolerable in its own right: a bloody nipple and vomited swallowed blood, compulsive latching, aversion described through scratching/hair pulling/punching a wall, and nipple touching that feels like ants. Do not describe all of these simply as a wish for time back. Body autonomy and sensory distress belong alongside pain and mobility.

## 4. Protected sleep and a workable method may be accommodations for psychiatric or sensory needs.

**Findings destination:** §9.1; §§6.1–6.2, 9.6 and 10; add a distinct accommodation/sensory-needs subsection.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Some people want responsive clinical or emotional support, some want to retain cosleeping”

Add prospective planning for eight uninterrupted hours where short sleep previously preceded mania, not only retrospective exhaustion. Keep the parent's bipolar account and the other parent's suicidal/care-relinquishment account distinct. Neurodivergent parents describe crying becoming overwhelming quickly; the parent of a toddler with an autism assessment rejects the suggestion that exhaustion will teach him to stop playing at night. Clearer wording alone is not the requested adaptation. The bouncer/running-shower arrangement is tied to the parent's own sensory regulation and psychiatric history, and its claimed safety is the speaker's belief.

## 5. Parents describe suicidal thoughts, self-harm, hopelessness and regret while trying to continue daily care.

**Findings destination:** §1; §§6.4 and 9.1; §10.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Mental health is central to some requests rather than a nearby angle.”

Retain the full severity: hearing voices/paranoia, suicidal thoughts, self-harm, regret at becoming a parent, disconnection from the baby, and considering inpatient help or handing care to relatives. Include appointments missed through exhaustion and medication declined because of waking, breastfeeding or a driving licence. Also preserve explicit rejection of a PPD explanation and the parent who says a large village still has not made this tolerable. These are reported states and explanations; no diagnosis or treatment effect is inferred.

## 6. Fathers report their own depression, suicidal thoughts, rage and physical distress.

**Findings destination:** §9.2 Fathers; §5.1; §9.1.

**Relation: CONTRADICTS THE NARROW FRAMING AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “They frame the pain through the wife:”

Replace that generalization with two visible strands: concern for the partner and fathers' own distress. The stay-at-home father describes loss of identity, depression and wishing he had been infertile; another reports past suicidal thoughts; another cracks teeth through clenching while anticipating the monitor. Several describe crying, overstimulation or yelling at the newborn. A plan a father can follow independently is one possible need, not an adequate portrait of this segment.

## 7. A child sleeping longer, a parent receiving time off, and a parent recovering are different outcomes.

**Findings destination:** §9.1, Their own sleep and Depression, anxiety, panic and loss of self; §§4.3 and 10.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “It does not support promising that the parent's own sleep will come back when the baby's does;”

Keep this qualification near any product benefit. Add a parent with suicidal thoughts despite an easy, sleeping baby; an eleven-hour sleeper whose daytime needs remain overwhelming; continued anger after better sleep and nights off; and a parent unable to sleep through the child's first eight-hour solo stretch who immediately sleeps when the child returns to bed. Time lying down can be dissociation, not restoration. Conversely, monitor reassurance or a partner taking all wakes can restore parental sleep; do not turn divergence into an assertion that sleep improvement never helps.

**Further supporting IDs:** [sbu03-0159] [sbu03-0174] [sbu03-0208] [sbu03-0209] [sbu06-0072] [sbu06-0088](#q-sbu06-0088) [sbu22-0152] [sbu22-0157] [sbu22-0265]

## 8. Settling accounts include reported hitting, rough handling, restraint and fear of losing control.

**Findings destination:** §9.1, Rage, resentment, regret and the parent’s ability to care; §§4.5 and 6.4; add explicit restraint/containment material.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “One parent describes yelling and slapping a child;”

Expand beyond a single slapping account. Preserve spanking and its immediate effect, a pushed child, harder-than-intended placement, pacifier/feeding handling, vigorous bouncing worries, and the parent's fear of becoming unsafe. Describe holding down/holding tightly for sleep or bedtime care as the authors do; do not relabel it all as cuddling. Containment includes tied tent zippers and a child escaping through a ripped base near an exposed staircase. The record also includes a parent interpreting vomiting as deliberate, which remains their interpretation.

**Verbatim evidence**

<a id="q-sbu03-0047"></a>
**[sbu03-0047]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0047 -->
> Today I’m watching my 4yr old and 18 month old nieces along with my 17 month old daughter while very pregnant. I was putting my daughter for a nap while my other niece was in the next room napping as well. My daughter starts screaming and refusing to go to sleep since we just transitioned her to her toddler bed. I didn’t think it would go bad since she did great in it last night. But here she is screaming and crying and I’m worried she’ll wake her cousin since her cousin is a huge screamer and a bit more difficult. I’m doing everything I can to make her stop screaming and it’s like a switch flipped. I got so cold. I stopped cuddling her and just taking her off of me and putting her into bed. I kept shushing her repeatedly. Telling her to stop. And then I spanked her. I literally spanked her. She immediately stopped and went down but I can hear she was still crying silently. She even reached out for my hand to hold mine which I did. And I pet her head to sleep but I feel like shit. I’m trying to hold it together right now but I can’t get it out of my head. She looked so scared.
<!-- /quote -->

> sometimes i have to bounce up and down with her while holding her paci in her mouth just to nap and im so scared that im giving her shaken baby. her head is always supported and i just bounce and bounce and bounce.

## 9. A second adult's presence does not guarantee a usable or trusted night handover.

**Findings destination:** §5.1, The night split includes health, conflict and coercion; §5.2; §§8 and 16.7.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “These are different from two willing caregivers needing the same script.”

Include repeated unannounced dozing, a baby reaching the floor, an adult who responds while apparently not fully awake, ineffective coffee, and a parent afraid even to shower. Include the first-person father trying and failing to stay awake, as well as the other parent's report. Medical accommodation can apply to the other caregiver too: the heart-attack survivor cannot obtain the uninterrupted sleep requested by a doctor. A paid agency nanny or nursery can lose trust after a specific incident; references and payment do not by themselves settle the parent's fear of sleeping while a stranger has access.

§16.7 says “It has to stand in for the person, on paper.” These accounts limit that inference: instructions do not supply a trusted, awake, physically able adult or prevent reported abuse. Separate clear instructions from actual respite and reliable care.

## 10. Some disputes around sleep involve reported domestic violence or intoxication rather than disagreement about methods.

**Findings destination:** §5.1, The night split includes health, conflict and coercion; §§6.4 and 9.6.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Disagreement also includes pressure to share a bed, disputes about the parent’s anxiety, a custody threat and a contested accusation of cursing at the baby.”

Keep a distinct account of the father reporting punching, kicking and blows with a Nintendo Switch during settling, and his fear that confrontation or separation could expose the children to harm. Add an abusive ex-partner as an unavailable childcare option; alcohol used secretly during childcare/bedsharing; a partner too drunk to respond; and a childhood abuser's unexpected presence during promised respite. Do not fold reported assault, substance use or fear of future harm into a partner-alignment script.

## 11. The accepted sleeping caregiver can vary by time of day, change suddenly, or be a nanny or grandparent.

**Findings destination:** §5.2 “Only mum can settle”, and the reverse; §§5.3–5.4 and 9.2–9.3.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Being held or sleeping beside a parent can work for the baby while leaving neither caregiver enough rest.”

Add the nanny demanded at 4am, nanny for naps/dad at night, grandparent preference that makes the mother feel rejected, and a father newly rejected only overnight despite daytime naps being fine. Include birth recovery shaping which parent became the original settler. The desired change may be flexibility for work, travel or illness, not removal of all assisted sleep. Preserve the account where offered relief is refused as well as accounts of absent or unreliable help.

## 12. Parents can want specific sleep changes while preserving feeding, touch, shared sleep or morning cuddles.

**Findings destination:** §5.5; §§6.2–6.3, 9.5 and 10.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Some value the closeness, some want to keep it while changing feeds or naps, some cannot sleep that way, and some reject both cosleeping and crying methods.”

Specify the mixed goals: keep a few minutes of bedtime patting but stop compulsory midnight bedsharing; keep morning bed snuggles after training; keep feeds while reducing long resettles; accept waking but want predictable days; continue bedsharing while moving naps. Add regret at missing shared sleep and longing for a past night holding a baby. Values can change with work, pregnancy, body limits and the child's preference; neither training nor independent sleep is a universal desired endpoint.

The opening also says “She does not want a philosophy.” That is too broad beside these explicit values, attachment beliefs and chosen forms of closeness. Replace it with the narrower observation that many parents want practical help that respects those values.

## 13. Bedsharing can improve rest, fail to settle the child, or let the child sleep while keeping the adult awake.

**Findings destination:** §5.5, Cosleeping is desired, endured, refused and reconsidered; §§9.1 and 9.5; §17.2.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Cosleeping is not a stable segment or a guaranteed source of rest.”

Keep favorable accounts beside the failures: movement, heat, latching, constant touching, play, bed size, falls and adult hypervigilance. Describe partial-night use separately from all-night use, and transfer success separately from staying asleep. An adult may bring the baby back solely to be able to sleep themselves. The detailed half-asleep blanket account shows why a decision made while fully awake may not survive the night; the speaker asks explicitly for harm reduction. Product language cannot assume either shared or separate sleep is tolerable to every family.

**Verbatim evidence**

<a id="q-sbu03-0053"></a>
**[sbu03-0053]**

<!-- quote:sbu03-0053 -->
> she had acid reflux and colic as an infant and could never sleep in a bassinet or crib. we has to hold her for every nap. she would wake in screaming horror after only a few minutes due to the reflux. we put her on medicine but her sleep was never good. she napped for only 25- 30 minutes on her own until 11 months. Nighttime was atrocious and she wouldn’t go in her crib at all. I resorted to co sleeping because that was literally the only way I could get some sleep. her naps started to get a bit better where she will take maybe an hour - an hour and a half once per day, sometimes twice. co sleeping is so exhausting for me because she constantly needs comfort throughout the night or is so restless hitting her head on the sides of the bedrail. I was waking all night to readjust her as it  was unsafe for her to be close to the bed rails. I made the switch to a toddler floor bed and we co sleep because of the safety issue. it’s just been so horrible. she’s now needing me for comfort all night basically. and if I even move my arm she’s awake. she wakes every hour And doesn’t ever seem like she’s in a deep sleep for more than 2-3 hours. I’m so exhausted, sleep deprived and just tired. I don’t like cry it  out. she’s a sensitive temperament and works herself up even worse when left to cry. so basically 14 months of sleep deprivation.

## 14. Bereavement, earlier loss and frightening events change the meaning of sleep and the support available.

**Findings destination:** §2; §§6.4, 9.1 and 9.6; §17.2; add grief/loss and frightening-event subsections.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “The triggers readers name, across sets: travel (often one night away), illness, teeth, standing and crawling, daycare starting, a sibling, a house move, a bed move, and dummy weaning at about two”

Add stillbirth, miscarriage, surviving-twin loss, parental/grandparent bereavement, family separation, parental hospitalization, storms/power failures, a distressing blood draw, a frightening costume and a wasp sting. Distinguish a parent's proposed cause from an established explanation. Loss can reduce the available village as well as alter risk tolerance: one parent rejects both training and bedsharing and calls the result 'team no-sleep'; a father who has barely slept after a prior stillbirth says he could not be happier. A widowed parent asks to be understood for the brief routine she can sustain.

## 15. Judgement and fear of intervention can change what parents are willing to disclose about sleep.

**Findings destination:** §3; §§5.5, 6.3–6.4 and 7; correct §17.6.

**Relation: EXTENDS AND CONTRADICTS A FACTUAL ABSENCE.** Current wording: “no police this wave.”

Replace the spring-wave absence claim with its scoped historical limitation: the previously selected wave-3 evidence had no police account, but this re-read adds a 2026-05-28 hospital dispute in which a parent says leaving would trigger police/child-removal action and police later visited home and left after seeing the baby asleep. This is distinct from neighbor-noise complaints. Add explicit consideration of lying to a pediatrician, a partner advising it, fear of CPS when requesting anxiety care, and reluctance to discuss sleep arrangements. A questionnaire cannot assume disclosure is easy or complete. Preserve the unvaccinated parent's complaint that others withhold sleep advice and parents' demands not to be diagnosed from a comment.

## 16. Method names do not specify the actual touching, feeding, waiting, picking up or stopping that families use.

**Findings destination:** §4.5, What stopping actually looked like; §§6.1, 10 and 17.6.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “A stop is not simply failure to persist.”

Describe the behavior, not just Ferber/chair/gentle/CIO labels. The quoted chair instructions explicitly permit brief pickups; a father's chair account describes intensifying crying and his interpretation of 'shutdown'. In another account crying happens while held, so the parent asks whether responsive settling itself counts as CIO. Include breath-holding/blue spells, hoarseness, choking/gagging/vomiting, crib injury, standing/sleeping upright and inability to sit back down. Keep explanations uncertain: later fever changes an initial interpretation of hoarseness, and another child loses her voice even without CIO. Sleep training and night weaning are not interchangeable.

> "STEP 1 (DAY 1-3):
> • once the 15-30 minute bedtime/wind- down routine is completed (bath, story,
> bottle/breastfeed, songs) and baby is in cot awake, sit on a cushion right next to the cot
> • if any fussing/crying occurs, put a still hand on their chest and shush loudly
> • avoid picking baby up. If baby hasn’t shown any signs of calming after 15-20 mins,
> pick up briefly and then place down again once calm but still awake, limit hold time to
> 1-2 minutes and place down immediately when calm or put baby down regardless after
> 2 mins even if still crying
> • stay until they fall asleep (wait 5-10 mins until they’re in a deep sleep) and then leave
> or go to bed if it’s night time and the cot is in your room."

## 17. Parents can match a schedule closely and still lack an explanation, and some do worse with less nap time or longer windows.

**Findings destination:** §4.4; §§6.2 and 7; §§17.2 and 17.6.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “A plan that only ever lengthens windows repeats the sub's blind spot”

Keep contrary responses to lengthened windows, nap capping and early bedtime. Add the feasibility problem when advice presupposes naps longer than the baby ever takes, and when a parent must wake the baby from every sleep to preserve the schedule. A nominal eleven- or twelve-hour night may include long crying/awake periods. A good night may include feeding, walking and rocking at 5am. Read later replies: an apparent nap-cap win is later discussed in terms of gas/reflux treatment; a next-night attempt can fail. Quiet split nights raise whether to intervene at all, not just how to enforce a target schedule.

**Verbatim evidence**

> Here’s our situation:  
> \- we sleep trained him using the chair method just before he reached 12M. We have a short routine, and then I sat in chair until he fell asleep, regardless of crying. He started sleeping through the night in just a few days.  
> \- we were doing approx 3/3/4 during the day (2 naps). Something like 10AM nap and 2:30P nap, with bedtime at 7:30P and wake up around 6:30A.  
> \- one confounding factor: his crib is in our room, and our bed creaks. We have a divider screen so he can’t see us.  
> \- he had a very shrill cry that is tough to stand more than a few minutes when we’re in the same room as him  
> \- starting a couple weeks ago, he started waking up between 5-5:30, making us sit in chair for an hour. Then it became two wakeups a night - with the first around 2A. If there is an early wake up, we’d just do diaper change, song, magic phrase and he would go straight to tummy and sleep.  
> \- since he’s almost 14M now, we moved him to one nap this week, around 11AM. He napped 1.5 hours the first day, and he slept through the night when we put him down around 6:45P, waking up at 5:45A! But the last three days have been tough again. His naps have been getting longer (first 2 hours, then 3 hours the last two days). And he’s gone back to waking twice a night, around 2A and waking up for good by 5:30A.   
> \- this past night, he slept at 7:16P, woke up at 4:27A. I laid in bed as he calmed and went to his tummy on his own. But 25 mins later, he was crying loudly. I waited a few mins and then reset him by doing a diaper change, song, magic phrase. But he the proceeded to cry at my for over 40 minutes very loudly as I sat in chair. He finally went to bed nearly 85 mins after he first woke up. And here I am sitting in the chair still wondering what to do.  
> \- I’ll also note that at the 5:30A wake days, I take him to the living room, and he’ll pretty quickly clamor for milk.

## 18. Sleep equipment transitions depend on size, motor skills, manufacturer limits, heat and real product fit.

**Findings destination:** §4.6, Gear and sleepwear; §5.4; §§8, 9.6 and 10.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Restoring them shows temporary wins, failures, temperature and daycare constraints, and repeated reluctance to buy another short-lived fix.”

Add rolling before reaching transition-sack minimum weight, unused bought swaddles, crib height despite no climbing, missing conversion parts, mattress gaps, caregiver reach, and sleep positions that differ by nap/night. Keep parents' modified Snoo setup and recognition of rolling risk visible, alongside Owlet reassurance that makes continued swaddling feel less frightening. Include the dog-bed mattress hack as reported advice, the laundry basket as an ordinary-object experiment, the traditional rocking crib, ghee, and temperature guides that conflict with the parent's observation. These are reported products, practices and beliefs, not validation of their claims.

## 19. Monitors and tracking can reassure, alarm, distort sleep arithmetic or change a parent's risk judgement.

**Findings destination:** §4.3; §§6.4 and 9.1; §17.6.

**Relation: EXTENDS.** Current wording: “Monitors can misclassify waking or generate repeated alerts;”

Add both positive accounts: peaceful parental sleep despite placement alarms, and repeat low-oxygen alarms followed by emergency assessment and hospital oxygen. Those are parent-reported experiences, not device-performance studies. Keep fear of monitor disconnection, uncertainty about recorded wakes, desire for exportable data/shared logs, and difficulty hearing distress through white noise. Reassurance can support a behavior the parent already wants, including continued swaddling, while others say a warning device cannot prevent SIDS. This share is primarily monitors/apps and internet advice, not new evidence that chatbot plans work.

## 20. Work, siblings, housing, travel and access determine which sleep changes are physically possible.

**Findings destination:** §§5.1–5.4; §§9.3–9.7 and 10; §17.2.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “These widen the fit requirement beyond age and willingness to follow the method.”

Add supervision of a toddler while settling an infant, simultaneous nursing/naps, adjusted ages and twins, sickness colliding with work deadlines, travel without a suitable advertised crib, heat and construction forcing room or accommodation changes, and no affordable safer housing. Daycare can work with cues while naps remain tiny and the child appears happy; its noise/routines can also block the household's intended changes. A parent asking for shorter settling does not necessarily need fewer wakings. Family planning is affected in both directions: preparing sleep for another baby and deciding another baby is unaffordable or unmanageable.

## 21. Success can mean easier settling, longer stretches, retained feeds or tolerable care, and these outcomes can diverge or change over time.

**Findings destination:** §2; §§4.1–4.5, 9.1, 16.2, 16.5, 17.2 and 17.7.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “these are observed sequences rather than proven causes or a universal restart protocol.”

Retain genuine relief and short or longer successes alongside setbacks. Feeding or rocking to sleep can coexist with uninterrupted nights; sleep onset independence can coexist with waking. Bedsharing may improve rest or fail; one child's sleep improves when a sibling arrives, contrary to a simple disruption story. A gradual month-long change preserves through-nights even when bedtime later takes seventy-five minutes: a partial outcome is not total relapse. An accidental crib transition leaves wake frequency unchanged. Do not infer that longer-window tweaks, a medical treatment, positioning or a method caused every apparent win.

## 22. Parents ask for concrete next steps, but the requested step may preserve current support or require a person.

**Findings destination:** §§6.1–6.3; §§10, 16.7 and 17.9.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “They ask for a plan for this child, written as steps, with a rule for when things go wrong.”

Keep the literal asks, including step-by-step bassinet transition, a two-nap schedule for a twelve-hour-total sleeper, what to change first, exact 5/3/3 feed timing, comforting through illness without losing progress, room-sharing methods, sleep advice for reflux/feeding aversion or blue spells, and how an exhausted father can stay awake. Requests also seek a full-size infant mattress in Canada, a cot with particular dimensions/fit, a monitor/app, medicines to discuss, a clinician or PPD support. These are different requests; do not count all of them as demand for a static digital plan. The Requests index below keeps each explicit ask available.

## 23. Purchases and value judgements concern gear, hands-on care and medical access as well as sleep information.

**Findings destination:** §§4.1–4.3, 4.6, 8 and 10; §§16.7 and 17.9.

**Relation: CONFIRMS AND EXTENDS.** Current wording: “acceptance of this specific plan and price is still untested.”

Keep the distinction between actual payment, prospective spending, affordability, comparative price and perceived value. This share contains a $35/hour agency nanny, a gifted mattress about $50 cheaper than the one requested, thousands spent pursuing relief, a contemplated £415 tongue-tie appointment alongside an ordered Zazu, and an approximately $500 full-bed budget. Include unpriced purchases, wasted formula, unwanted weighted sleepwear, expensive secondhand sleepers, therapy affordability, housing/childcare costs and paid-private treatment. No quoted amount establishes willingness to pay for Mamivio's proposed document; no refund or money-back experience appears in these four files.

<a id="q-sbu20-0394"></a>
**[sbu20-0394]**

<!-- quote:sbu20-0394 -->
> LO started off with a runny nose but was herself (this lasted a week). Then all of a sudden super high fevers, sleeping truly all the time, no appetite, not wanting to play. Truly just wanted to be held and breastfed. Started to get really worried on day 3 of fevers so we brought her to the GP who figured she had an ear infection. Prescribe antibiotics and we started taking it that same morning. Day 4 the fevers finally broke but nothing else changed. She was sleeping an insane amount of time. She could only be awake 1-2hrs then needed to go back to sleep.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="q-sbu20-0395"></a>
**[sbu20-0395]**

<!-- quote:sbu20-0395 -->
> Then day 5 full body intense rash appeared so we rushed her to the hospital. Then we got the Roseola diagnosis and Dr cleared her from ear infections (told us to stop taking antibiotics). Rash lasted 3 days. Now we’re officially 1 week since the start of her fevers and 2 weeks since the runny nose. Physically she looks back to normal but she’s still SO clingy, crying a lot and not eating much in terms of solids. She’s also sleeping more than her usual.
<!-- /quote -->

## What fits nowhere

The current findings have broad neighboring sections for these subjects, but no dedicated place for these specific mechanisms. These are proposed additions to the outline, not exclusions.

### Parent sensory regulation can organize an improvised sleep environment.

The current health and gear sections have no dedicated account of a parent's long-standing running-shower sensory routine becoming the baby's bathroom/bouncer sleep arrangement. Add this mechanism rather than calling it merely inconsistent routines.

- [sbu22-0014](#q-sbu22-0014) — full quotation above (pattern 4).
- [sbu22-0015](#q-sbu22-0015) — full quotation above (pattern 4).
- [sbu22-0016](#q-sbu22-0016) — full quotation above (pattern 4).
### Grief changes both the goal and the village.

No current dedicated section combines pregnancy loss, surviving-twin loss, bereavement, a widowed parent's brief bedtime, or two very different reactions to sleeplessness after stillbirth. Add these to the arc and household constraints without treating them as a uniform psychology.

- [sbu06-0011](#q-sbu06-0011) — full quotation above (pattern 14).

### Disclosure itself is part of the service-design problem.

Judgement is already covered, but the explicit choice to lie, fear of CPS when seeking help, and hospital/police account require their own treatment under professional encounters. This is not a neighbor-noise story.

### Containment and restraint need their own account.

The current headings cover crying, harm and rooms but not the physical mechanics of holding a resisting child or tying a tent zipper in unsuitable housing. Do not erase the behavior by putting it only under parental exhaustion.

The partner section acknowledges coercion but has no distinct treatment of a father reporting blows with a console, other reported forceful handling, secret drinking, or promised respite involving a feared childhood abuser. These belong as reported events, not as a communication tip.

## Money

Every explicit financial signal and purchase/anticipated purchase identified in this share is indexed here, including medical access, childcare, accommodation, contraception, gear, wasted formula and gifts. Linked follow-ups are retained for value/usability context. Product ownership alone does not establish purchase. Free time, sleep budgets, body weights, “worth a shot,” and time spent are not money amounts.

This comprehensive navigation index retains direct product, plan, schedule, list, service, clinician/medication and practical-care requests, together with adjacent advice/reassurance questions and ambiguous/rhetorical uncertainty. The latter are labelled separately and are not asserted to be requests for a paid plan. The table descriptions are navigation summaries from record contexts, not substitute quotations. Linked IDs point to full quotations above; all IDs remain locatable in the four source files.

Requests distinguish accepting one or two feeds from wanting no wakings, keeping bedside support from ending it, retaining shared sleep from changing only naps, and needing a clinician/awake helper from needing a document. Some expressly reject CIO, bedsharing, generic sleep hygiene, earlier parental bedtime, or diagnosis from a comment. Keep those stated limits with the ask.

## Verification and coverage

Read coverage was audited by row position, independently of non-contiguous source IDs: UR03 1–394, UR06 1–332, UR20 1–394, UR22 1–304, with no gaps or overlaps. The requested comparison sections in all four ledgers were read. Current-conclusion excerpts were checked as exact substrings of the current findings.

Every mentioned ID belongs to these four files. Every full quote block is generated directly from its record’s quote field. The validator removes only Markdown blockquote prefixes and requires exact equality, including punctuation, typos, internal/trailing spaces and line breaks. No network, git, source edits or other file writes were used.

Coverage: quotes in the share **1,424**; quotes reproduced verbatim **619 unique quote records** (619 full quotation blocks); further IDs listed without reproducing their quote **805**; IDs not mentioned at all **0**. Verbatim checks: **619/619 passed**; mentioned IDs: **1,424/1,424 valid**.
